- **The temperature RIGHT NOW is the second-largest thing on screen**, read from the weather entity's state rather than the forecast: today's high shown that big is wrong for most of the day.
- **Every colour on the board is a token.** Zero hard-coded greys remain outside the browser-only setup panel, which is what makes `panel_theme: light` actually work here instead of producing the half-broken light rendering the other pages still have.

What this is NOT: the three heavy embeds (a real map in the map tile, the Drives timeline, a one-day calendar agenda) are still summaries **at this version — they land in v2.125.0**, and the other eleven pages still have the incomplete light-theme mapping. The visual language is expected to keep iterating; this establishes the vocabulary it iterates on.

## Light mode, the orb, and the shelf that isn't there (v2.114.0)

From two photographs of the running panel — "dark mode is pretty good, light mode needs work" — plus two live notes.

- **Light mode is not dark mode with the colours flipped**, and shipping it that way is why it looked bleached. Dark glass works because a translucent dark card is always darker than a photo; a translucent *white* card on a *white* scrim is the same value as its own background, so the cards vanished. The contrast had to move the other way: the scrim dropped from 74%→34% white (the photograph keeps its colour) and cards rose from 68%→88% (they read as paper laid on it), with a real shadow and a stronger hairline.
- **Member colours are picked once, on a dark UI, and then used everywhere.** On white, Lily's pale blue and James's yellow all but disappeared. `tone()` keeps the HUE — that is the person's identity — and clamps only the lightness into a band that reads against the current surface (≤38% on light, ≥66% on dark). Nobody has to re-pick a colour per theme, and Nathan's deep purple stopped reading as dim on dark at the same time.
- **The theme is applied in `<head>` from a cached value** and corrected when the profile lands, so a panel never flashes dark and settles light. It is set on *every* panel page now, not just the board.
- **Fixed from the same photographs**: the mosaic tiles were clipping past their rounded corners (no `overflow: hidden`), cards stretched to the tallest in the row so "where everyone is" was six names above a large empty box (`items-start`), and an untracked member rendered as a name followed by nothing, which reads as a rendering failure rather than "not tracked".
- **The Argyle bar is an orb.** A black slab lying across the bottom of a display for the 99% of the day nobody is talking to it was the most intrusive thing on the board. It collapses to a 3.25rem orb at rest, opens on a tap, and closes on send, on Escape, or on a tap anywhere else. It breathes slowly — available, not demanding — and not at all under `prefers-reduced-motion`. `?chat=none` still wins outright.
- **The shelf has no background at all.** The content fades out on its way down behind it, via a **progressive blur**: one fixed strip whose `backdrop-filter` is revealed by a `mask-image` ramp, so text dissolves into the photograph instead of being chopped at an edge. Two obvious approaches are wrong here and are guarded by tests: masking the scrolling content is sized to the **document**, so the fade lands at the bottom of the page rather than the screen (and the mask would capture the fixed shelf and fade the buttons with it); and a solid gradient to "the page colour" cannot work when the page colour is a photograph. The buttons carry their own text/drop shadow since they now sit on an unknown image. The overflow flyout keeps its glass — it is a menu, and a list has to be readable.

`tests/test_panel_chrome.py` guards the resting state: kiosk is switched on before anything reads the URL, one shelf height feeds everything that must clear it, the bar is collapsed at rest, and the shelf stays background-free with the fade a sibling rather than a child.

## The skin reaches every page, and every page can have its own picture (v2.115.0)

- **A look that stops at one surface is worse than no look.** The home board shipped its aesthetic and nothing else got it, so tapping DRIVES on the shelf dropped you out of a photographic display into a flat dark web page — which makes the other ten pages read as broken rather than plain.
- **Rewriting eleven templates was not the answer.** `templates/panel_skin.html` does what `ha_theme.html` has always done for Home Assistant themes — map the Tailwind greys the pages are *already written in* onto a small token set — except the tokens are ours and carry light/dark with them. A page written a year ago in `bg-gray-800` becomes glass on a photograph without being touched. Surfaces map onto a glass scale (900 quiet, 800 card, 700 raised), ink onto `--panel-fg`/`--panel-dim`, edges onto `--panel-line`, and form controls get an explicit rule because an input on a photograph is otherwise indistinguishable from a caption.
- **Everything is scoped to `html[data-panel]`, deliberately.** In an ordinary browser the admin pages stay exactly as they are. Silently restyling every page of a working app for people sitting at a laptop is a much bigger promise than the one being made, and it is unverifiable from here. A test asserts every mapping rule carries the panel scope.
- **Colour that means something is left alone.** Accent buttons, status pills and member chips keep their own colours; washing them into the token set would flatten exactly the information they carry.
- **`panel_page_backgrounds` gives any page its own picture** — a highway behind the driving schedule, the kitchen behind meals — keyed by nav slug, same grammar as the default (a URL, or a phrase to look one up). A page absent from the map uses `panel_background`. The whole resolved map travels in the panel profile, so moving between pages costs no extra request: the panel already has the answer before you tap. Unknown slugs and blank entries are dropped rather than stored.
- **The background layers are injected by `nav.html`**, not added to eleven templates, and `nav.html` is the single owner in panel mode (the board only drives its own background in a browser). Page slugs come from the same `NAV_ITEMS` the shelf is built from, so "the drives page" means one thing to the background chooser, the shelf and the tabs filter alike.
- **`config.html` never included `ha_theme.html` at all** — found by the test that asserts the skin reaches every page. It therefore had no Home Assistant theming either, which was an omission rather than a decision, and now has both.

## The theme break, the flash, and the orb in the middle (v2.116.0)

- **THE BUG that broke every page.** The v2.115 grey mapping applied `backdrop-filter` to the mapped surfaces. A filter — backdrop or otherwise — makes an element a **containing block for every fixed-position descendant** and opens a new stacking context. Several pages (`config`, `map`, `app`) carry `bg-gray-900` on **`<body>` itself**, so the mapping put a filter on the body, which captured the shelf, the orb and the two `z-index: -2` background layers — trapping the photograph behind the very element it was meant to sit behind. Blur is now confined to `.panel-card`, which is applied deliberately to leaf cards containing no fixed chrome; the mapped surfaces use their own, more opaque tokens (`--panel-surface-1/2/3`) precisely because they have no blur to lean on. The body is explicitly excluded from the surface mapping and has `filter: none` forced.
- **The second half of the same break**: `html[data-panel] body` is transparent so the photograph shows through, but nothing carried the base colour, so the canvas was **browser white**. On ordinary pages that showed as white bands; on the board, where a dark scrim sits over it, white read as a flat mid-grey. `html[data-panel]` now carries `--panel-bg`. Both failures are pinned by tests, with the containing-block reasoning written out — it is not obvious from the symptom.
- **No more flash of the browser chrome.** The panel/browser switch is CSS in the head now, not JS at DOMContentLoaded: `data-panel` is set synchronously before the body exists, so `#top-nav-bar` is hidden and the shelf shown at the first paint. Previously the desktop nav painted, sat for a frame or two, then vanished — the single most visible tell that this was a web page wearing a panel costume. It also means the panel no longer depends on `nav.html`'s script completing in order to look right.
- **Two more flashes of the same kind, closed the same way.** The shelf would have shown all twelve destinations before snapping to six, and the photograph dropped in after the first frames. Both are now decided in the head: the URL wins when it says anything, otherwise the last known value (tabs list, and the resolved picture for *this* page) is read from cache and applied before anything renders. `nav.html` re-applies the real answer when the profile lands and refreshes the cache — and removes its own head-time guess first, since a broad `:not(...)` rule would otherwise outrank the per-element answer.
- **The orb opens from the middle.** It shares the chat bar's `translateX(-50%)` axis and a bottom transform-origin, so tapping it reads as one thing growing into the bar rather than a swap between two corners. The collapsed state keeps the centring transform, or the orb would slide to the left edge on its way out.
- **`/` lands on the home board** rather than the driver schedule. An address with nothing after it is asking "what is going on", which is the board's whole job; the schedule was the right answer only while it was the whole app. The query string survives the redirect, so a panel pointed at the bare host still arrives in panel mode.

## One line of JavaScript that took the whole panel down (v2.116.1)

- **The bug**: `nav.html` called `panelProfile()` near the top of its `DOMContentLoaded` handler, while `let _profile = null;` sat two hundred lines below it. Function declarations hoist; **`let` does not** — it stays in the temporal dead zone until its own line runs. The call threw `ReferenceError` and took the rest of the handler with it: no shelf, no tab filtering, no background, no idle return, and the desktop nav bar left sitting on a wall panel.
- **Why nothing caught it.** It is valid syntax, so `node --check` passes. Every template renders, because the failure is in the browser. The panel profile endpoint returns 200. The symptom that finally exposed it was oblique — *"the background works in regular mode but not in panel mode"* — because the home board sets its own picture in a browser, while `nav.html` owns it in panel mode, and `nav.html` had already died. The earlier "top nav is showing and there's no shelf" screenshots were the same crash, and the CSS-in-head fix from v2.116.0 had masked most of its visible effects without addressing the cause.
- **`tests/test_nav_runtime.py` executes the script** against a DOM stub thin enough to be honest about what it fakes, and asserts the handler runs to completion in panel mode and that the background actually gets applied. Verified by reintroducing the bug and watching it fail. There is also a cheap source-order guard naming the exact hazard, so a future edit that moves the declaration back down fails immediately rather than at the next photograph of the wall. The test skips rather than fails where node is unavailable.

## Every page says its name in the same place (v2.117.0)

- **The page background was covering the photograph.** You could see the picture behind the shelf, where the fade is transparent, and nowhere else. `bg-gray-900` is Tailwind's *page* background and the pages put it on the wrapper that fills the content area — so mapping it to a surface laid a 62%-opaque slab straight over the image. Only **800/700/600 — the actual cards — are surfaces now**; 900/950 and their opacity variants are transparent. The calendar's hand-rolled `.glass-panel` is pointed at the card token rather than its own hard-coded slate, since it is the one page-level container that is genuinely supposed to be glass.
- **Titles were in eleven different places.** Some pages had none, most used `text-3xl font-bold`, Occasions used `text-2xl font-black`, Trips a 4xl gradient — and each sat in a container of its own max-width, so walking the shelf moved the title around the screen on every tap. Two classes fix it in one place: `.panel-page-title` is the name, `.panel-page` is the frame. **Chores & Rewards was already the right shape, so it is the reference.**
- **Added where missing**: Map, Calendar and Schedule. Map and Calendar are full-height pages, so the title goes in a `shrink-0` header *above* the content rather than overlaying the one part of the page that is all content. Schedule's title was buried in a sticky toolbar in its own size.
- **The frame is only forced full width ON THE PANEL.** In a browser a reading column is still the better shape for these pages, and widening them there is a change nobody asked for.
- **Renamed for the shelf**: the errands page said "Inbox" while the button said ERRANDS, and Trips said "My Trips". A destination should be called one thing.
- **`home` is deliberately exempt** and the test says so: the board's identity is the clock, and a page title above it would be a label on the one surface that never needed one. `config` and `settings` are exempt as admin surfaces the panel hides anyway.
- A test walks every page template and fails if one ships without a title or without the frame — it caught Moments, which had the title class and no frame.

## Glass on every page, and the calendar's cage of white lines (v2.117.1)

- **The panes were opaque.** `--panel-surface-2` shipped at 78% with no blur, so the driving schedule's timeline, and the big containers on Errands, Meals, Occasions, Chores, Routines and Moments, were flat slabs sitting on the photograph. Dropped to 55% (dark) / 72% (light) **and given their blur back**.
- **Why the blur is safe now, when it broke everything in v2.115.** It is on the CARD tiers only — 800/700/600 — and never on the page tiers 900/950. A filter makes an element a containing block for fixed descendants, and the pages that put a grey on `<body>` use 900 or 950; **none uses 800**. The panel's fixed chrome all sits as a direct child of `<body>`, so nothing above it filters. That condition is now checked against the templates rather than asserted in prose: a test fails the day a page puts a card-tier grey on its body, because that is the moment the shelf and the background layers quietly break again.
- **FullCalendar draws its grid from its own variables**, defaulting to a light `#ddd` — over a photograph, a cage of white lines that no amount of Tailwind mapping touches, because the borders are not Tailwind. `--fc-border-color`, `--fc-page-bg-color`, `--fc-neutral-bg-color` and `--fc-today-bg-color` now point at the token set.

## Cards and wrappers are not the same grey (v2.117.2)

- **The split is by USAGE, not by shade**, because this codebase uses `bg-gray-900` for both. Bare `bg-gray-900` is a **card** — the driving schedule's driver columns, the dashboard's rule sidebar — so it is glass. The opacity variants (`/30`…`/90`) are the page-level **scroll wrappers** that fill the content area (errands, moments, settings, routines), so they are transparent and let the photograph through. Getting this backwards put a slab over the picture one way (v2.117.0) and would have deleted the schedule's panes the other.
- **`.panel-page` joins the transparent set**, so a page frame never paints over its own background.
- **The blur now sits on every mapped tier**, and the whole scheme rests on one override: `<body>` forces `filter: none`. `config` and `map` put `bg-gray-900` on their body, and a filter there is a containing block for every fixed descendant — the shelf, the orb and both background layers. The test asserts that override and each neutralised body tier by name, because it is the single line holding the design up.
- **The calendar sets its own `!important` day backgrounds** (`.fc-day`, `.fc-day-today`) in its page CSS, from before any of this existed. Token mapping cannot reach an `!important` rule, so only a more specific `html[data-panel] .fc-day` override wins. That, plus the four FullCalendar colour variables, is what clears the grid.

**Deployment note**: several of these fixes reportedly showed no change on the panel. The screenshots that came with those reports had no page titles on Schedule or Calendar — titles that shipped in v2.117.0 — so that build predated the fix being reported on. Confirm the add-on version in the footer/`config.yaml` before diagnosing a CSS change as ineffective; the add-on store caches the repo and needs *Check for updates* → *Rebuild*.

## Two complete themes were fighting, and HA was winning (v2.117.3)

- **The actual cause of the opaque panes**, tracked down from the browser by reading the winning rule: `body.ha-theme .bg-gray-900 { background-color: #111111 !important }`.
- **Panel mode was switching HA theming on as a side effect.** `?panel=true` writes `kiosk=true` into the address so the app's existing kiosk gating lights up — and `isHaTheme` was `(theme=ha…) || kiosk`. So every wall panel also got the HA mapping. On a *standalone* panel there is no parent frame to sync colours from, so `--ha-bg` never resolves and its **fallback becomes the theme**: `#111111`, opaque, over the photograph.
- **It beat the panel skin on a technicality.** `body.ha-theme .bg-gray-900` and `html[data-panel] .bg-gray-900` have *identical* specificity (one class-equivalent plus one more, plus an element), and both are `!important` — so the later stylesheet wins, and `panel_skin.html` was included *above* the HA styles. Three rounds of tuning opacity values could never have fixed this, because the rule was never being applied.
- **Fixed twice over.** Panel mode now suppresses HA theming outright (`isHaTheme = !isPanelTheme && …`) rather than trying to out-specify it — two complete themes cannot both be on, and the panel carries its own light/dark. And `panel_skin.html` is now included **last**, so a future HA rule cannot outrank the panel simply by being added further down the file. Both halves are pinned by a test.
- **The lesson worth keeping**: when a CSS change appears to do nothing, the next step is to find the rule that is actually winning, not to adjust the rule that is losing. The user did that with devtools in one step after three of my speculative rounds.

## The gradient that outranked the photograph (v2.117.4)

- **Calendar and Trips showed the gradient, never the picture.** Both give the body a `background: linear-gradient(...)` in their own CSS, and the panel skin only cleared `background-color` — which leaves the gradient's **background-IMAGE** untouched. It then covers the photograph, because `#panel-bg-image` sits at `z-index: -2` and negative layers paint *before* the body's own background. The body rule now clears the shorthand and `background-image` explicitly.
- **`trip.html` never included `ha_theme.html` at all**, so it got no `data-panel` attribute and not one panel rule could reach it — which is why it stayed fixed-width with a gradient-text title while everything else changed. It now has the include, the `.panel-page` frame and `.panel-page-title`, and it is **no longer exempt** from either the skin test or the title test. `config.html` had the identical omission (found in v2.115.0); these two were the last.
- **Trip tiles were a screen and a half each.** Three columns on a 2500px panel is an 800px-wide card, and `aspect-[4/3]` makes that 600px tall. Now up to five columns with `aspect-[16/10]` and a height cap.
- A note for the next time a rule "does nothing": two of the last three theme bugs were a *losing* rule, not a wrong value — an `!important` at equal specificity from `ha_theme`, and a shorthand that only looked cleared. Read the computed style and find the rule that wins before touching the one that lost.

## A field that is its own container (v2.117.5)

- The Argyle bar is **one textarea inside a rounded shell**, and the panel's input mapping gave every field its own surface — so it drew a second, differently-coloured box inside the first: a pale slab in light mode, a darker one in dark.
- **`.bg-transparent` is the app saying "this field IS its container."** Those fields now keep only the ink and the caret; everything else still gets a surface, because an input on a photograph does need to look like an input. General rather than a special case for the chat bar — any field the app deliberately made transparent is left alone.

## The trip page joins the rest of the app (v2.118.0)

- **The hero banner is gone.** It carried the trip's photograph, which made the trip page the one surface that did not look like anything else. The trip name is now the page title in the same place as every other page, and **the trip's photograph is the page background** — handed to the shared fixed layer, so it gets the scrim, the crossfade and the theme for free.
- **Everything the banner carried moved, and is named in a test**: the way back to the Trips grid (critical — `?tabs=none` can hide the nav's Trips button entirely, so an HA card could open a trip the family then could not leave), the settings gear, the location, the dates, the countdown and the notes. A banner is where things accumulate; a test that names each one is cheaper than rereading the diff later.
- **The trip page's own background layers stand down on a panel.** `#bg-img` and `.gradient-overlay` predate the shared layer and sit at the same `z-index: -2`, so they stacked with it at 40% opacity and turned the trip's own photograph into a wash. They still do their job in a browser.
- **The map page's map is inset with rounded corners**, like the trip page's. Edge-to-edge made it look like the window rather than something *on* the page, and it ran under the shelf's fade at the bottom.

## The trip page the panel actually renders (v2.118.1)

- **`/trip` serves `trip_kiosk.html` whenever `kiosk=true`** ([main.py](chauffeur/main.py) `trip_view`), and panel mode sets `kiosk=true`. So the wall panel renders the KIOSK trip template — and v2.118.0's entire rework of `trip.html` was invisible there. The page looked untouched because, on that surface, it was.
- Everything v2.118.0 did to `trip.html` is now done to `trip_kiosk.html` as well: the hero banner is a standard page header, the trip name is the page title, the photograph is the page background through the shared layer, and the frame is full width. The "All trips" link, location, dates and countdown all moved with it — the way back matters most here, since `?tabs=none` hides the nav on exactly this surface.
- **The test now checks BOTH templates.** Checking one of a pair where the other is what actually ships is precisely how a whole release went unnoticed, so the scenario loops over both and names the template in every failure.
- Worth remembering when a change "does nothing": before assuming a stale build, check whether the surface being looked at is served by a *different template*. Two of these now exist — `trip`/`trip_kiosk`, and the kiosk variants generally.

## No page title carries an emoji (v2.118.2)

- Occasions (🎉), Moments (📸) and Intake (📥) were the only three of eleven with an icon in the title. Three of eleven is worse than all or none: the icon shifts the first letter right, so those titles did not line up down the left edge with the rest — the exact thing the shared title class exists to guarantee. The shelf already carries an icon for every destination, so the title was saying it twice.
- Guarded by a test that scans every `panel-page-title` for emoji, rather than relying on the next person to notice.

## The shelf is an ordered list (v2.119.0)

- **The shelf buttons could be picked but never ordered.** `panel_tabs` was already a list and `resolve_tabs()` already preserved its order — nav.html collapsed it into a `Set` at the door (`applyTabs(new Set(p.tabs))`) and rendered the shelf in whatever sequence `NAV_ITEMS` happens to be written in. So the setup UI offered a bag of chips while the tiles beside it offered ▲▼✕, and the only way to move a button on a wall panel was to edit a template.
- **`applyTabs()` now takes the list**, and `orderShelf()` walks it, parking each button before the More button — `insertBefore` moves a node it already contains, so the row ends in exactly the picked order with anything unpicked (and hidden) left at the front, where `layoutShelf()`'s display filter already skips it. Overflow follows for free: the order IS the choice of which destinations stay under a thumb and which go behind **More**.
- **Only the shelf reorders.** The desktop row and the mobile menu are ordinary web chrome read left to right by someone already looking at a screen; the shelf is reached for by position by someone standing in front of a wall.
- Slugs are looked up through a `Map`, not a `[data-slug="…"]` selector — they can arrive from `?tabs=` on the URL, and a stray quote would throw out of `querySelector` and take the rest of the handler with it (a plain object would have the same problem with `__proto__`). `?tabs=schedule,chores` now orders an embedded HA card's shelf too.
- **`/home#panel-setup` gives shelf buttons the same editor as the tiles**: a numbered ordered list with ▲▼✕ and a row of unpicked chips to add from.
- **One visible consequence for installs that never configured a shelf**: `DEFAULT_TABS` order now reaches the wall, so the default shelf reads home, drives, calendar, chores, routines, meals, errands, occasions, trips, map, moments rather than nav.html's declaration order.
- `tests/test_nav_runtime.py` grew a real shelf row — children, one parent per node, honest `insertBefore` move semantics — because ordering and overflow are things the script does BY moving nodes, and the old stub swallowed every move and would have agreed with anything. The profile it asserts against is deliberately not in declaration order.

## The board fills the wall (v2.119.0)

- **The home board was capped at `max-w-[1600px] mx-auto`.** Every other page is a document and reads better in a column; the board is a WALL, and a 2500px panel spending 900px of itself on two empty margins is a smaller board with a bigger television around it. The page padding is now the only inset.
- **Four tiles across** (`sm:2 lg:3 xl:4`). Three was the right number under the 1600px cap; edge to edge it made 500px-wide tiles carrying a four-item list, and the board ran off the bottom of the screen rather than filling it. The ladder still steps down — a 10" tablet in portrait gets one column, not four columns of nothing.
- **Tile widths go to 4** in `panel_tile_spans` (rows stay at 3). A grid four wide whose tiles top out at three is a full-width layout the household can see but cannot ask for. Clamped in `spanStyle()` as well as the picker, because it is stored config.

## The tiles line up (v2.119.1)

- **`items-start` let every card shrink to its own content**, so a row of four ended on four different lines — and a tile the household had given two rows drew itself one row tall with a hole underneath, which reads as a bug rather than as the size somebody chose. The grid is `items-stretch` now: tiles fill their row, content stays top-aligned inside.
- The trade is real and taken deliberately: the shortest card in a row carries some empty space. A ragged bottom edge costs the board its shape, and shape is what you read from across a room; slack inside one card costs nothing at that distance.

## Serves did not save, and neither did anything else on a dish (v2.119.2)

- **`set_dish_fields` read `req.whole_units` off a model that had no such field.** v2.109.2 added `whole_units` to `MealPatch` and taught this handler to read it, but `DishFieldsReq` — the model this endpoint actually takes — never got the field. Pydantic raises `AttributeError` for a field it was never given, and the read is **unconditional**, so every call 500'd *after* building its patch and *before* writing it.
- **It was never only `serves`.** All four corrections on the dish row — serves, the whole-meal switch, the by-the-tray toggle, the category chips — go through that one handler, so the hand path for what a dish IS has been dead since v2.109.2. The agent writes the same fields through a different door, which is why voice kept working and why nothing looked wrong from the model, the route table, or the tests.
- **Guarded by a scan, not a memory**: `tests/test_request_models.py` walks every handler in `main.py`, finds each `req.<attr>` read on a parameter typed as a request model, and checks the attribute is a declared field (pydantic's own surface excepted). 79 models, one bug — and the same slip in any future endpoint now fails at the test rather than at the wall panel. Plus the round trip itself, and the clamps: `serves` is a scaling input for every timing and shopping number downstream and this endpoint is reachable from a browser.
- The lesson generalises past pydantic: **a capability with two doors needs a test on the door that is harder to open.** The voice path exercised these fields constantly and proved nothing about the screen.

## Two spans, and a list that can be taken back off (v2.120.0)

Reported from using it: *"It only generates meals up until the next grocery run... we don't start building the next wave of meals until after we have been to the grocery store, so we miss the chance to buy the stuff to make those meals."* Half right, and the wrong half mattered — `plan_window` already planned past the shop, but only inside the **lead window**, two days before the trip. See `docs/meal_week_design.md` §M10.

- **The horizon was binary and opened late.** For five days of a seven-day cycle there was no next-shop plan at all, so an idea landing on a Monday had nowhere to go and the list could not accumulate against the coming run. And planning mode STARTED the window on the shop date, so the last nights before the trip belonged to neither span and vanished off the page. `plan_window` now returns **both spans, always**: `current` (today → the trip, what the last shop bought for) and `next` (the trip → cadence, what the coming trip buys for). `start`/`days`/`mode` still point at the span being bought for, which is what every caller meant by them.
- **The un-add had to come first**, because a plan you can edit for six days is a plan you buy for twice. `ShoppingItem.claims` records EVERY night that wants a row. The old bug is why removal was impossible rather than merely unbuilt: `dishes_to_shopping` **skipped** an ingredient already on the list, so chicken wanted by Monday and Thursday was one row remembering only Monday. A duplicate now leaves a claim instead of being waved away.
- **The rule: an ingredient stays while any planned night still wants the dish that brought it.** Matching by DISH and not by (dish, night) is load-bearing — *"plans change at the drop of a hat... generally that meal would just get punted to a different day and everything else shifts"*. Per-night matching would strip the noodles the moment lasagna moved from Thursday to Friday. Under dish matching a night punted across the shop boundary arrives already paid for, and it does work itself out.
- **Three things are never removed**, each a different way to throw away real shopping: a row a PERSON added (not a consequence of the plan, so not undone by it), a row already CHECKED OFF (you own it now — the top-up case), and a row carrying NO claims (a pre-claims meal item; unexplained is not unwanted).
- **Batch writers reconcile once, at the end.** Moving a dinner is two writes and the dish is planned nowhere in between — reconciling in that gap unbought a dinner the family had merely dragged. `_persist_plate(..., reconcile=False)` plus one call, used by `arrange_week`, `approve_week` and `repropose_week`.
- **Runs are per ITEM, not per list** (`ShoppingItem.buy_on`). Binding a list to a run contradicts M1's standing-list decision for a reason still true — the errand regenerates every cycle while the list persists across all of them, and a list per run is how a second list rots while everyone keeps adding milk to the main one. `buy_on` is the run BEFORE a night, not the next one on the calendar; a night falling before the next run is a **top-up**. `shopping.item_runs` splits the open list into now / next / later, and the rest is offered rather than hidden — standing in a store is the best moment to be asked, and buying some of it just checks those rows. One group renders no header at all.
- **`grocery_cadence_days`** (default 7) replaces the hardcoded span. Seven is right for most families; a household shopping every ten days had three nights a cycle nothing bought for.
- Hand paths: two labelled sections on the meals page with nights draggable across the shop boundary, run groups on the list, the cadence in Planning and shopping. Voice: `get_shopping_list_items` reports the split when there is more than one run (shared by both agent stacks). Tests: `tests/test_meal_runs.py` (11 scenarios) — including the three never-remove guards and the punt that must cost nothing.

## The padlock changed the dinner (v2.120.1)

- **Clicking lock on a week night swapped its dinner for one further up the page.** `compose_plate` ranks a date in ISOLATION; `compose_week` composes the span in order, threading what each earlier night took — which is the entire reason a week is not the same top-ranked entree seven times. `set_plate_lock` read the isolated answer, so it froze the plate that night would have had *if the week started there*: the first night's dinner.
- **It was not a display bug**, though it looked like one. `set_plate_lock` persists what it locks (`edited=True`), so the wrong dinner was genuinely stored — which is exactly why the night then offered a Reset. Anything locked before this needs a look.
- **Three other buttons had the same bug**, because they all composed the night in isolation before writing it: `pin_plate`, `add_to_plate`, and `remove_from_plate` — the last of which was handed a dish list the night was never showing, so dropping a chip reported "unchanged" and froze a different dinner on the way past.
- `meals.dishes_showing_on(date)` is now the one answer to "what is this night showing", and every path that FREEZES a night goes through it. It reads the span containing the date and composes up to it. A date outside both spans (a birthday three weeks out, reached through "plan a specific night") has no week context, and the isolated composition is exactly what that picker showed — the one case where isolation is right.
- Worth remembering: composing in isolation and composing in sequence agree for the FIRST night and disagree for every other one. Tonight's plate is index 0 in its span, which is why this never showed up until a week strip existed to click a padlock in.

## A trip already under way kept beginning (v2.120.2)

- **"Camp Kesem begins! 🎉" — on day four of five, to both kids, every morning.** A `background_trip` is cut into one event per day for the UI and the solver, and the slicing loop only creates a slice for a date **inside the solve window** (`if date_str in fetched_by_date`). The days before the window opened are never sliced at all. `_kid_trip_line` reconstructed the trip's shape from the surviving slices, so the earliest one always looked like day one — and the line is chosen by `target == first.date()`.
- **The slice now carries the whole trip.** `Event.span_start` / `Event.span_end` are stamped at slice time from the original event, before the loop throws its dates away. It is the only source that survives the window; the old slice scan stays as the fallback for caches written before this, and is still honest-but-short rather than a guess.
- **An off-by-one came with it.** An all-day calendar event ends at midnight of the day AFTER its last day, so a literal reading is a trip one day too long with a homecoming on a day nobody travels. The slices used to hide this — each was clipped to its own day (`min(e.end, day_end)`) — so taking the real span for the first time exposed it. `_kid_trip_line` now folds an exclusive-midnight end back onto the prior day, the same convention `status_protocols` already spelled out.
- **`status_protocols.set_days_from_calendar` had the same defect** and is fixed by the same fields. Its comment already said the rule it was breaking — *"a 5-day trip must become ONE 5-day span, never five one-day instances"* — which the solve window was quietly violating from the other end.
- Worth remembering: **anything reassembled from slices can only see what the window contains.** Three separate readers now strip `_slice_` to regroup (`meals`, `presence`, `status_protocols`); regrouping is safe, but inferring the ORIGINAL's extent from the parts is not.

## A fix that only worked after the next refresh was not a fix (v2.120.3)

- **v2.120.2 shipped and the wall still said "Camp Kesem begins!"** The new `span_start`/`span_end` are written by the schedule refresh, so every slice already in the cache had neither — and the fallback for those was *the old scan*, which is the bug. The correct behaviour arrived only after the cache was rewritten, which is not something the family does or should have to know about.
- **The rule: never announce an edge you cannot prove.** `_kid_trip_span` returns a third value, `trusted`. It is False when the answer came from the scan AND the trip's slices run to the edge of what the cache holds — the window is what clips, so a span touching that edge may have more trip behind it. An untrusted span renders `🏕️ Camp Kesem — away`, which is true whatever the real dates are. "begins!", "day 3 of 5" and "coming home" are all claims about edges, and a kid reads them as facts about their day.
- **Suppression still uses an untrusted span.** A clipped span covers fewer days than the trip but every day it does cover is a day the kid is genuinely away, so home events during it stay hidden. Only the announcements need certainty.
- The general shape, worth remembering: **a fix that depends on data written later is dormant until then, and its fallback is the behaviour being fixed.** Either migrate the data or make the fallback safe. Here the second was right — the cache rewrites itself on the next refresh anyway, and "away" costs nothing while it waits.

## The two edges of a trip are known separately (v2.120.4)

- **v2.120.3 stopped the lie and threw away the truth with it.** One `trusted` flag covering the whole span meant a kid on the LAST MORNING of camp was told only "away" — when the fact they wanted was that they are coming home today, and that fact was sitting in the cache the whole time.
- **The window clips the two ends differently, so they are asked about separately.** The past is clipped hard — days before the window opened were never sliced at all — while the far end is only clipped by a trip running past it. The ordinary state of a camp already under way is therefore: nobody can prove when it started, everybody can see when it ends. `_kid_trip_span` returns `start_known` and `end_known`, each true when something else in the cache sits further out than that edge.
- **Every line now needs only the edge it is ABOUT.** "Coming home" needs the end and does not care when the trip began. "begins!" needs the start. A day count (`day 3 of 5`) is the only line needing both — and it is the least valuable of them. With just the end: `🏕️ Camp Kesem — home tomorrow`, which is the thing a kid is actually counting down to. With just the start: `day 3`, no total. With neither: `away`.
- Worth remembering: when a fact is uncertain, ask what part of it is certain before falling back to silence. The first cut of this collapsed an answerable question into an unanswerable one and called it honesty.

## Cutting the inference back out (v2.120.5)

- **The family's question, and it was the right one: the slicer is holding the original event, so why is anything being inferred?** It isn't any more. `span_start`/`span_end` are the original's own dates copied onto every slice (v2.120.2), and that is the whole fix. The two versions after it built an inference layer for slices cached *before* those fields existed — reading how far a trip's fragments sat from the edges of the cache to guess whether the window had clipped them.
- **That bridged a five-minute gap.** `poll_schedule` re-runs the refresh every 300 seconds and rewrites the entire event payload, so a cache without span fields is minutes old at worst. A page of careful edge reasoning, two extra flags and four extra line variants, all to be slightly better during a window that closes itself.
- **`exact` is one boolean now.** True when the dates came off the slice. False only for a pre-fix cache, where the line is just the trip's name — no day number, no beginning, no homecoming. Suppression still uses an inexact span, because every day it covers is a day the kid is genuinely away; only the LINE needs exact dates.
- Worth remembering: when the cure for stale data is more reasoning about the stale data, check how long it stays stale first. Here the answer was five minutes, and the reasoning was the most complicated part of the feature.

## The span was dropped one step before the code that wanted it (v2.120.6)

- **`member_day._ride()` builds its ride dicts from a WHITELIST of keys**, and `span_start`/`span_end` were not on it. The trip span was stamped onto every slice, carried through the refresh, written to the cache — and then thrown away at the last hop, so `_kid_trip_line` never once saw it. That is why the wall said "🧳 Camp Kesem": the no-dates branch, on every trip, forever.
- **Three fixes in a row could not have worked**, and the tests could not see it: every scenario handed `_kid_trip_line` a hand-made dict shaped like a *cached event*, never like a *ride*. A test that does not cross the seam cannot see the seam. The regression test now runs end to end through `_build_kid_digests` → `member_day`, and with the two keys removed it reproduces the exact line from the wall.
- One older scenario (`test_kid_school_hours.scenario_trip_digest_lines`) had been passing on the OLD slice-scan path — its three slices were the whole trip, so reassembling them happened to give the right answer. It seeds the span now, like the refresh does.
- Worth remembering: **a whitelist is a silent contract.** Adding a field upstream does not add it downstream, and the failure is not an error — it is the absence of a fact, which reads as "we don't know" rather than "someone dropped it". When a value has to travel, test the journey, not the departure.

## A board row is a real unit (v2.121.0)

- **"2 rows tall" guaranteed nothing.** The tile grid sized its rows to content, so a span of 2 meant "as tall as whatever those two rows happened to be" — a height decided by the OTHER tiles in them rather than by the household. And in the **last row it did nothing at all**, because there was no second row there to occupy. Reported from the wall.
- **`grid-auto-rows` is the whole fix**, and it is the model Home Assistant's grid uses: pick what one row is worth and a tile's height is its span times that. `grid-auto-rows` covers IMPLICIT rows, which is exactly what the bottom of the board is made of — so a 2-row tile in the last row is now genuinely two tall.
- **`panel_grid_row_height`** (default 120px, clamped 60–400) is set on the meals... on the home board's own setup card, and travels in the board payload so the page never guesses a number the server also knows. Spans go to 4×4.
- **One deliberate departure from HA: the row is a FLOOR, not a ceiling** (`minmax(Xpx, auto)`). HA's cards are built to a fixed height and clip; these are content summaries, and a board that silently cuts off the third of four drives is the precise failure the hide-nothing-that-is-merely-quiet rule exists to prevent — you cannot tell a clipped tile from a broken one. A tile that outgrows its allotment pushes its row instead of hiding what it has to say. Every default tile is 1×1, so a strict ceiling would have started clipping the whole board on day one. `minmax(Xpx, auto)` → `Xpx` is the strict version if the household would rather have exactness.

## The floor made the grid a no-op (v2.121.1)

- **v2.121.0 changed nothing on the wall, and the reason is arithmetic.** The row was a MINIMUM (`minmax(120px, auto)`), chosen to protect tiles from clipping. But a tile's content is already taller than any sane minimum — the board's tiles run 250–300px — so every row went on being sized by content exactly as before, and a 2-row span came out at 256px: roughly one ordinary row. **A floor cannot make anything twice as tall.** A span multiplies only when the row is a ceiling as well as a floor, which is precisely why Home Assistant's grid is built that way.
- **`grid-auto-rows: <h>px`**, strict. Default 240px (about what a tile is now, so a 1×1 board looks close to unchanged) and a 2-row tile is 496px — visibly double, in the last row as anywhere else.
- **Paired with scrolling, not clipping.** A tile that outgrows its slot scrolls (`overflow-y-auto`, scrollbar hidden — a scrollbar on a wall is furniture). The board must never silently drop what it has to say; the honest fix for a tile that scrolls is another row, and now that rows are real the household can give it one.
- Worth remembering: **a safety valve that makes the feature inert is not a safe default, it is a broken one.** The reasoning for the floor was sound in isolation and wrong in the only context that mattered — the actual heights of the actual tiles. Check a design against the real numbers before shipping it as the cautious option.

## Columns are a real unit too (v2.121.2)

- **Only rows had been fixed.** Columns were still Tailwind's `grid-cols-4`, so one column was a quarter of the board and **a quarter was the narrowest thing anybody could ask for** — no half-column, no third, nothing smaller. The column count looked identical before and after v2.121.0 because it was.
- **`panel_grid_columns`, default 12** — Home Assistant's number, for the same reason: it divides by 2, 3, 4 and 6, so halves, thirds, quarters and sixths all exist. Tile widths are spans of these. Tracks come from CSS custom properties rather than Tailwind classes, because the count is the household's and Tailwind only ships classes for the numbers it knows (and the CDN build only generates what it can see in the markup).
- **Narrower screens get half then a quarter of the columns** (`--c-md`, `--c-sm`). A span wider than the grid is **clamped** by grid auto-placement rather than overflowing, so a quarter-width tile is a half at 6 columns and full width at 3 — proportional collapse, for free.
- **Both axes are number fields now.** A 12-column grid has 12 widths and a dropdown listing them is a menu that grows with the setting. The width field carries the fraction beside it (`of 12 · a quarter`), because the number you type and the thing you meant are different.
- **`migrations.migrate_tile_columns_v21212` multiplies stored widths through** (×3 for 4→12), so a tile that was half the board stays half rather than silently becoming a sixth. A tile sized only vertically gets an explicit quarter, which is what it was already being drawn at. Idempotent, keyed on app state.
- An unsized tile defaults to **a quarter of the grid**, not one column — otherwise every tile nobody had touched would have become one-twelfth on upgrade.

## The mosaics fill their tile (v2.121.3)

- **A picture tile's mosaic was a fixed stack of 4.75rem rows**, so making the tile taller just added a band of empty card under the photographs — on the one kind of tile where size is the entire point. The mosaics now take `grid-auto-rows: minmax(0, 1fr)` inside a flex column, so their rows divide the height the tile actually has.
- **Picture tiles are a column, and they do not scroll.** `isMosaic()` names them (`meals`, `moments`, `trips`) rather than detecting an image, because a tile that happens to contain a picture is not the same as one whose whole job is showing pictures. Scrolling a photograph is not reading it, and a mosaic that fills its slot has nothing below the fold by construction.
- **The layout tiles now.** The old rule promoted the first item to a 2×2 leader, which looked right against fixed rows and left holes the moment the mosaic had to fill a whole tile — a 3-up in three columns put the leader across two rows and left two empty cells beside it. The rule is now: one item takes everything; the LAST item widens to close whatever the final row has left over. Verified to leave zero empty cells for 1–9 items at both 2 and 3 columns.
- Worth remembering: **a fixed-size child inside a resizable parent is a bug waiting for the parent to resize.** Three of these landed in one arc — rows, columns, and now the mosaics — and every one was a number chosen when the container only had one size.

## The caption moved the thing you were aiming at (v2.121.4)

- The tile width field carried a live fraction beside it (`of 12 · a quarter`). It was a **different length for every value**, so the rest of the row's controls shuffled sideways under the hand that was changing them. A caption that moves the target costs more than it explains. It reads `cols` now, like `rows` beside it.
- Worth remembering for any live-updating label sitting in a row of controls: if its width tracks its value, it is a layout shift on every keystroke. Either give it a fixed width or do not put it inline.

## The schedule page was deleting its own name on a panel (v2.122.0)

- **Reported from the wall: the title and controls flash up and vanish.** Two faults in one `if`. `dashboard.html` hid `#page-header` whenever the surface was read-only — and panel mode sets `kiosk=true`, so every panel got it. `#page-header` carries the **title**, so the schedule page had been quietly deleting its own name on every panel since before the one-title-per-page convention (v2.117.0) existed. On a panel that heading is the only thing on screen saying which room you are in: the wordmark is gone and the shelf is icons.
- **And it hid them from `DOMContentLoaded`**, so the server sent the controls, the browser painted them, and a frame later they disappeared. That flash is what the family actually saw.
- **A display surface is now known in the HEAD.** `ha_theme` sets `html[data-display]` for `?panel`, `?kiosk` OR `?readonly` — before anything paints, for the same reason the theme is decided there — and one CSS rule hides `#header-buttons`. The page's JS no longer touches either element. An HA card and a read-only embed get the same no-flash treatment the panel does, which they did not before.
- Guarded by `test_panel_chrome.scenario_a_page_never_hides_its_own_name_on_a_panel`, which scans every template for a page hiding its own header and fails if the head stops marking display surfaces.
- Worth remembering: **hiding chrome after the first paint is always a flash.** Three separate things now decide before paint (theme, shelf tabs, controls); anything else that reads the URL to decide what NOT to show belongs there too.

## The shelf arrives in its final order (v2.122.1)

- **v2.119.0 made the shelf order real and applied it in JavaScript**, once the panel profile arrived — so the family watched the buttons shuffle on every page load. My flash, introduced by the fix two arcs ago.
- **The server already knew the answer.** `main._shelf_order(request)` is registered as a Jinja global and is the same `resolve_tabs` the profile endpoint returns; `nav.html` builds `SHELF_ITEMS` from `NAV_ITEMS` in that order and renders it. The first byte is correct, for the stored profile AND for `?tabs=` (the server reads the query string too). `orderShelf()` stays as a no-op safety net for a card that edits its own URL without reloading.
- **The head-time tab guess is gone with it.** It filtered the shelf from a cached list to avoid painting twelve buttons and snapping to six; server rendering is both earlier and correct, and the cache could only contradict it — a stale entry would hide a button the server had rightly drawn until the profile arrived. `?tabs=none` stays in the head, because that hides the whole nav rather than choosing its contents.
- **A new destination is still one entry in one list.** `SHELF_ITEMS` is derived from `NAV_ITEMS`, and the nav parity test now checks the derivation rather than the literal loop.
- That is four things now decided before the first paint: the theme, the background, the page controls, and the shelf. The pattern is worth naming — **if the server can answer it, the server should render it; JavaScript that corrects the DOM after load is a correction the family watches happen.**

## A page is configured in one place (v2.123.0)

- **The panel setup listed the same twelve destinations twice**: once to pick and order the shelf buttons, and again, in a collapsed `<details>`, to give each page its own picture. You chose Meals in one section and scrolled to another to say what the meals page looks like. They are the same thing — a page the panel shows.
- **The picture rides on the page's own row now**, the way a tile's size rides on the tile's row. One list: order it, remove from it, add to it from the chips, and set each page's photograph where the page already is.
- **A page dropped from the shelf keeps its picture.** Making room for Chores must not silently discard the photograph somebody chose for the meals page — put it back and it is still there. The honest cost of one list instead of two: a page that is not on the shelf has nowhere to edit its picture. Tiles can still open such a page, so this is a real (small) loss, taken deliberately.
- The board-wide background keeps its own field and is now described as what it is: the picture behind every page that has not been given its own.

## Ink on a photograph is not page ink (v2.123.1)

Two light-mode readability faults from the wall, one root cause: the skin maps Tailwind greys to the panel's ink, which is right for text on a SURFACE and wrong for text on a PICTURE.

- **Trip card captions went dark on a dark scrim.** A trip card is a photograph with a scrim ramped over its lower half; its title and dates are a caption on that scrim, not page text. Mapped as page ink they followed the theme and turned dark grey in light mode — over a scrim that is dark in both themes. They are forced white on a panel now. A caption over a scrim is white in both themes, because what is under it is dark in both.
- **The scrim was deepened to earn that white.** The card's own gradient fades to 20% opacity by 60% of its height — enough over the dark page it was drawn for, not enough over a bright photograph, which is exactly where the titles sit. Panel-only, so the browser card is untouched.
- **The cart chips had no surface at all**, so there was nothing for the skin to map: an outline and a word floating on a bright sky. Fixed with `bg-gray-800` in the app's own grey vocabulary rather than a panel-only override — the skin maps it to glass and the browser keeps its dark chip.
- Worth remembering: **the grey mapping assumes a surface underneath.** Anything drawn over an image needs its own answer, and anything with no background class is invisible to the mapping entirely.

## Light mode lightens the picture (v2.124.0)

- **v2.123.1 fixed the trip captions by forcing them white, and the family's next report was the real problem**: the image-backed blocks *"feel like they are not light mode… they feel darker than the rest of the page"*. Correct — a dark scrim is a dark surface however light the room is, so meals and trips read as holes punched in a light page.
- **So light mode inverts the whole treatment.** White band, dark ink, white text-halo: the block becomes paper laid on the photograph, like every other card in the theme. `.96` opacity at the text and clear at the top — near-opaque where the words are, because dark ink over an unknown photograph is only safe on a band that has neutralised it, and still a picture above that.
- **One token pair for all three places that put text on a picture**: `--photo-scrim`, `--photo-ink`, `--photo-ink-dim`, `--photo-ink-faint`, `--photo-shadow`, defined for dark, light and `auto` under a light device. The trips grid, the meals week strip and the board's mosaics each arrived with their OWN gradient and their own hardcoded white — which is why the first fix reached one of them and left the other two.
- **`.photo-scrim` rides alongside each page's existing gradient** rather than replacing it: an `!important` stylesheet rule outranks an inline style, so the browser keeps exactly what it had and only the panel is re-themed. No page lost its non-panel appearance to this.
- Worth remembering: **"make the text readable" and "make the block belong" are different problems.** The first was solved by forcing white and looked fixed; the second is what the family actually saw, and it needed the scrim to change colour, not the ink.

## Three tiles stop being summaries (v2.125.0)

The last item outstanding from the v2.112.0 review, and the one the board's own notes said needed *"a real component extraction rather than a summary"*: the map tile drew a list of zone words, the drives tile drew a list of times, and the calendar tile drew a flat list of the next six things. Each was a table of contents for a view that already exists elsewhere in the app.

- **The map tile is a map.** `components/family_map.html` was one map bolted to one pair of element ids held in file-level globals — right for a page that shows a map, useless to a board where the map is a tile. The drawing moved to **`components/family_map_core.html`**, a factory: `FamilyMap.create(el, opts)` returns an instance bound to whatever element it is given. `family_map.html` is now that factory bound to the page's two elements and keeps its four global functions, so `/map` and the PWA Map tab are untouched. One renderer, two callers: the marker a member gets on the tile is the same marker, from the same code, as the one on `/map`.
- **The tile's markers come from the board payload, not from a second fetch.** `_tile_map` was already reading every member's Home Assistant state and keeping only the zone word; it keeps `latitude`/`longitude` now, so the pins are **free**. Cars with a device tracker join at one state read each, as they do on `/map`. The payload deliberately speaks `/api/family/locations`' vocabulary (`member_id`, `latitude`, `driving: {leg_title}`) so the shared renderer takes either source with no translation layer between them.
- **`interactive: false`, and `pointer-events: none` over the top.** On this board every tile is a door, and a Leaflet map swallows the drag and the tap the door depends on. The tile is a picture of where everyone is; touching it opens the real one. OSM's raster tiles are dimmed on a dark panel — `/map` may be bright because you went there, a tile nobody asked for should not be the brightest thing in the kitchen at ten at night.
- **Chips carry what a pin cannot.** Anyone Home Assistant knows only by zone, *and* anyone driving — pin or not, because "en route to practice" is the more useful half and a marker cannot say it. When nothing has coordinates at all the chips are the whole tile, which is the old list, honestly.
- **The drives tile is a timeline**: a lane per driver, time running down the tile, every drive a block as tall as it is long. *Superseded one version later (v2.126.0): it was a timeline the board drew ITSELF, and it did not look like the page it was summarising — the renderer is shared now.*
- **The calendar tile is the calendar's AGENDA view**: a card per day, its events under it, the quiet days present and empty. A flat list of the next six things cannot say that Thursday is empty, and "Thursday is empty" is a thing families read a calendar to find out — the day cards say it by existing, which is the same reason the agenda view is built that way. Cards flow into as many columns as the tile is wide (`auto-fill`, `minmax(9rem, 1fr)`), so the same markup is one day stacked on a quarter-width tile and a working week across a full one. This is the tile that most rewards being made bigger.
- **Today is the one day that is filtered.** A card headed Today listing this morning's finished appointment is a card about the past, and it would put the calendar tile at odds with the drives tile about what is behind us — the exact fault v2.110.2 was written to close. A trip's own span event is excluded too: it covers every day of the trip and would print on all five cards, and trips have their own tile.
- **An unassigned event is coloured for it** (red, "⚠️ needs a driver"). It is the one thing on the tile that somebody has to act on, and leaving it the same grey as everything else was the tile declining to say so.
- **`fillsTile()` joins `isMosaic()`.** A map or a timeline is laid out INTO its tile the way a mosaic is, so both get the tile's full height and neither scrolls — scrolling a timeline hides the hour you are standing in. Named separately from the mosaics because they are not pictures.
- **Tested** in `tests/test_home_board.py` (28 scenarios; three new: the map carries pins in the shared vocabulary, the drives window always contains now and is never shorter than three hours, the agenda keeps its quiet days and drops this morning) and a new **`tests/test_home_board_runtime.py`** (5), which executes the board's helpers in node against a stub DOM — the same technique `test_nav_runtime.py` uses, and for the same reason: a timeline block is a `top`/`height` pair in percent, and getting the sign wrong draws an empty box with no error anywhere.

## One renderer, or it is not the same view (v2.126.0)

Three notes from the wall, on the version that was supposed to make these tiles real.

- **"The driving schedule does not look quite the same as the one on the actual page — timeline is different, event chips are different."** Correct, and the fault was the approach rather than the drawing: v2.125.0 gave the tile a timeline *of its own*, built from the board payload. A second drawing of the same thing drifts by construction. **`renderSchedule` moved out of dashboard.html into `components/schedule_timeline.html`** — 1,300 lines, verbatim apart from one correction — and the tile calls it. Same columns, same condensed dead time, same chips, same drive-time pills, because it is the same function.
- **The extraction was small at the seams and large in the middle.** The renderer's only outside dependencies were nine variables (`currentData`, the edge maps, `driversMap`, `isReadOnly`…) and eight functions, six of which are the page's interaction handlers. The state moved WITH it and lives in the component now; classic scripts share one global scope, so dashboard.html goes on filling those names exactly as before. The handlers get `window.x = window.x || noop` fallbacks that the page's own declarations overwrite — a surface that only displays the timeline gets no-ops rather than a ReferenceError from a chip somebody tapped.
- **The one correction: `isReadOnly` and `isKiosk` are options now**, defaulting to the page's globals. The AI-options modal has passed `isReadOnly: true` since it was written and the option was never destructured — it was read-only only because the page it lives on usually is. The board's tile is the first caller for which that is not true, and a wall panel must not reassign a drive by being leaned against.
- **The tile is fed from the board payload, never from `GET /api/schedule`.** That endpoint SAVES a combined range cache and kicks a background refresh; a panel polling it every minute would keep the solver warm forever for a display nobody is looking at. `home_board._schedule_slice()` builds one day out of the cache the board already holds, pruning the edge maps (`edges[driver][event]`) to the same day's ids — about 1 KB. `ai_metadata`, `duplicate_groups` and `solving_dates` are deliberately left out: every one of them renders a BUTTON.
- **The tile scrolls, and opens on the next drive.** It is the only drawn tile that scrolls, because the alternative is scaling the page's typography down to a few pixels — and a day drawn whole otherwise opens on breakfast. `next_event_id` comes from the same `over` rule the hero uses. **After the last drive it stays up** (v2.143.4): the tile used to swap to "Nothing left to drive today." — the hero's own sentence, repeated across a quarter of the board over an otherwise empty box. Now `next_event_id` goes `None` once everything is `over` and the panel rests the timeline at the bottom of the day; the `{'empty': …}` branch remains only for days with no drives at all ("No drives on the schedule today." / "Waiting for the schedule to be built.").
- **"The agenda view squishes each day down very narrow."** It was flowing as many days as it had into whatever width the tile happened to be. **A day card is three of twelve board columns now** — a quarter, the same unit a tile is measured in — so widening the tile adds day cards instead of thinning them, and the days that do not fit wrap onto the next line. The count comes from the calendar tile's own span (`agendaCols()` → `--a-lg`/`--a-md`).
- **How many days is a setting** (`panel_agenda_days`, default 5, clamped 1–14 like the calendar page's own agenda), edited on `/home#panel-setup` beside the tile it fills — the number only means something next to the width of that tile, and both are on that screen.
- **"The map doesn't need to list everyone. It should just be the map."** The chips are gone. The one thing still drawn over it is a sentence when there are no pins at all, because a grey rectangle is indistinguishable from a map that failed to load.
- **Tested by DRAWING it** (`tests/test_board_render.py`, 5 scenarios): the rendered page is loaded into **jsdom** with Alpine and a stubbed `/api/home_board`, and the assertions are about what is on the screen — two event blocks in the tile, zero draggable chips there, two draggable chips on the Drives page, four day cards across a full-width agenda, no list under the map. It found the bug that made it necessary: the renderer touches `#inbox-container`, which belongs to the Drives PAGE, and `null.classList` took the whole render down — leaving a tile headed THE REST OF THE DAY over nothing, which on a wall is indistinguishable from a quiet evening. Guarded now, along with the AI banner. The test **skips** where jsdom is not installed (it is not a project dependency), in the same spirit as `test_nav_runtime`'s node check.
- **`tests/test_schedule_timeline.py`** (5) guards the extraction itself: exactly one renderer per page, no page redeclaring what the component owns (a stray `let currentData` is a SyntaxError that aborts a whole page script — and Jinja renders it happily), every including page still parses, the read-only option honoured to its last use, and neither the board nor the component ever naming the schedule endpoint.
- Worth remembering: **"consistent with the page" and "a smaller version of the page" are different builds.** The first is a shared renderer; the second is a copy that starts drifting the day it ships. The family spotted the difference in one look.

## A thing that has started is not next (v2.126.1)

- **Photographed off the wall at 1:53 PM: "NEXT UP · 53 min ago — Pre Jazz/Ballet"**, for a class that began at one o'clock and still had ten minutes to run. Both halves were computed correctly and the sentence they made together was nonsense.
- **Same root cause as the v2.110.2 bug, in the other direction.** `live` is a MANUAL flag — somebody tapping a leg as started — and nobody taps. `over` was made clock-based for exactly this reason; "under way" had been left on the flag. So `todays_runs` now carries **`underway` = start ≤ now ≤ end**, computed from the clock like its sibling, and the hero prefers a run that is on over a run that has not begun.
- **Three states, and the board says which one it is in.** Under way → **"Happening now"** with *"ends in 7 min"*; not started → "Next up" with *"in 25 min"*; over → it drops out and the next thing becomes the hero, counting down again. The panel re-checks `underway` against the BROWSER's clock every second rather than trusting the field until the next poll, or a class starting at 1:00 would still read "next up" at 1:00:59.
- **`minutes_left` rides alongside `minutes_until`.** Once something has begun, how long it has left is the useful number; how long ago it started is a question nobody in the kitchen is asking.
- Pinned twice: `test_home_board.py` with the exact times from the photograph (1:53 PM, on, then 2:01 PM, moved on), and `test_board_render.py`, which reads the words "Happening now" and "ends in …" out of the rendered DOM.

## The day still reads as a day (v2.126.2)

- **The agenda tile dropped today's finished events; the calendar page's own agenda greys them and always has.** Two views of the same day disagreeing about what a day contains is the same fault as two drawings of the timeline, one layer down.
- **They stay, at 45% opacity** (`past` on each row, set from the same clock reading `over` uses; an all-day event is never past, because half of it is still ahead). The stated reason for dropping them — "a card headed Today listing this morning's dentist appointment is a card about the past" — was wrong in the way that matters on a wall: it also makes a busy morning invisible, so a card showing two things at four in the afternoon reads as a quiet day rather than as a day nearly done.
- **When a day does not fit, what goes is what has already happened**, from the top, oldest first, counted as "+2 earlier" above the rows. Trimming the tail instead would drop this evening's pickup to keep this morning's school run, which answers the wrong question.
- Pinned in `test_home_board.py` (the whole day on the card, in order, with the finished one flagged) and in `test_board_render.py`, which reads the greyed rows out of the rendered DOM by class and checks that exactly the past ones carry it.

## The panel brings its own emoji (v2.126.3)

- **Reported from the Raspberry Pi on the wall: the emoji in the tiles do not render.** Nothing in the app was wrong — **Raspberry Pi OS ships no colour emoji font**, so every 🚗 🍽️ ⭐ in the board, the shelf, the kid digests and the status chips came out as a tofu box. A phone and a laptop have one built in, which is why it only ever showed up on the panel.
- **Two things have to be true and only one was.** Tailwind's DEFAULT sans stack already ends in `"Apple Color Emoji", "Segoe UI Emoji", "Noto Color Emoji"` — but *every page in this app* overrides `fontFamily.sans` with `['Inter', 'sans-serif']`, which drops all three. So `panel_skin.html` requests **Noto Color Emoji** from Google Fonts and puts the families back on the end of `body`, `.font-sans` and the mono stack (a monospaced tofu box is still a tofu box).
- **Display surfaces only** (`?panel=true` / `?kiosk=true`, resolved server-side in the same file). Not for the weight — Google serves this face in `unicode-range` slices, so a page pulls the two or three its emoji fall in — but for the family NAME: Android's own emoji font is *also* called "Noto Color Emoji" and an `@font-face` beats a system font of the same name, so every phone in the house would download a font it already has. iOS and Windows match their own earlier in the stack and never ask.
- The override that caused this is the trap a new page will fall into again — it is one line in every template's Tailwind config — so the fix is central and `tests/test_panel_chrome.py` guards all three halves of it: the font is requested, the request is gated to a display surface, and the families are actually in the stacks.
- **If the panel ever has to work offline**, this is the piece to vendor into the add-on. Tailwind, Alpine, Leaflet and Inter all come from a CDN too, so today it is not the weakest link.

## The board and the Drives page were reading different caches (v2.126.4)

- **Reported from the wall**: an event for today changed, the schedule re-solved, and the board said there was nothing left to drive — while the Drives page, two taps away, listed the drives. It came back on its own a few minutes later.
- **Two caches, one of them written far less often.** `GET /api/schedule?start&end` — what the Drives page and the PWA use — assembles its answer from the **per-day rows** (`get_cached_daily_schedule`), which the solver rewrites the moment it finishes a day. `storage.get_cached_schedule()` — the global combined cache, and until now the home board's only source — is written by `refresh_schedule_logic` **only when it is called with no date range** (`if not start_date_str and not end_date_str`). A single day re-solving never touches it. The five-minute poller (`poll_schedule`) is what eventually repairs it, which is exactly the "wait a few minutes and it fixes itself" that was observed.
- **`home_board.day_schedule(day)` reads the day's own row first** and merges it over the global cache — for every day the board can show, not just today, because a wall that disagrees with itself by a few minutes per day is not better than one that disagrees by five. **This cannot go the other way round**: the global cache is *compiled from* those rows, so a daily row is never the staler of the two.
- **The merge prunes by the whole DAY, not by the ids the daily row happens to list.** An event the re-solve dropped is absent from that row, so keying on its ids alone would leave the old assignment behind — a driver's name on the wall for a drive nobody is doing. Assignments, ghost/car assignments, `unassigned`, `no_location` and the three edge maps (keyed `edges[driver][event]`, pruned one level down) all follow the same drop-then-overlay.
- **Events are a UNION, deliberately.** A daily row holds the events the solver was *given*; an appointment nobody drives to is on the calendar tile and never in there. The cost is that a cancelled event lingers in the calendar tile (and as an unassigned column on the timeline) until the next full refresh — a much milder wrong than the day looking empty, and it heals on the same five-minute cycle.
- **"No drives today" and "there is no schedule" are now different sentences.** An empty cache — fresh install, cleared caches, the minute before the first refresh lands — says *"Building the schedule…"* in the hero and *"Waiting for the schedule to be built."* on the tile. The confident version of a claim you cannot support is how a wall board stops being believed; this is the third time that lesson has been paid for on this surface.
- Pinned in `tests/test_home_board.py`: the merge takes the day's own copy of an event, drops an assignment the re-solve took away, prunes the edge maps with it, leaves the global cache untouched when there is no daily row, and keeps the two empty states distinct.

## The hero says when to leave (v2.127.0)

- **Asked for from the wall**: *"it would be nice if the hero said 'leave at 4:20pm' or 'leave in 1 hr 46 min' instead of just telling me the event starts in 2 hr 26 min — the leave time is the more important of the two."* Correct: the start time is when somebody else expects you; the departure is the one a family sets an alarm by, and the board was printing the wrong one of the two big.
- **The number already existed and was walled in.** The solver computes the travel into every drive it assigns, and the kid digest has printed "🚀 Leave by 8:47 AM with Vovo" since arc K5 — but that arithmetic lived inside `main.member_day`, which a service cannot import. It is **`services/leave_by.py`** now, and `member_day` calls it: one definition of "leave by", or the kitchen and the phone eventually disagree about when to put your shoes on.
- **start − travel − the driver's buffer**, from either edge the solver produces: an **initial** edge is the driver setting out FROM HOME (which is what "leave by" means to somebody standing in their kitchen), a **route** edge is them arriving from wherever they already are. The board takes both; `member_day` passes `from_home_only=True` and keeps its own narrower meaning, because a child reading "leave by 4:20" is being told when to put their shoes on. Errands carry their own `travel_to_mins` instead of an edge.
- **No travel time, no leave time**, and the hero silently falls back to counting down the start. A guessed departure gets a household burned once and then the whole board is never believed again — the same rule K5 shipped with.
- **The pill counts down to the departure** ("leave in 1 hr 46 min" → "leave now" → **"should have left 6 min ago"** in red, which is the one state on this board worth shouting about), and the big right-hand number is the departure with *"for 5:00 PM · 26 min drive"* under it. Once the drive is under way the leave time is irrelevant and the start takes the big slot back.
- Pinned in `test_home_board.py` (4:20 from a 5:00 start, a 26-minute drive and a 14-minute buffer; a mid-chain drive gets a real departure; a drive with no known route gets none) and in `test_board_render.py`, which reads "leave in 1 hr 46 min" and "4:20 PM … for 5:00 PM · 26 min drive" out of the rendered DOM.

## Follow the sun — the panel theme outside Home Assistant (v2.135.0)

- **Reported from the house**: the panel tracked the household's sunrise/sunset theme automation perfectly *on an HA dashboard*, and was stuck in light mode forever when the same panel was opened through the Cloudflare tunnel that serves the PWA.
- **Why, exactly.** `auto` was never "follow Home Assistant" — it is the CSS `prefers-color-scheme` media query, and it only tracked HA by accident: HA's dark theme sets `color-scheme` on the embedding document, and Chromium propagates that into a nested frame. Remove the embedder and the query falls back to the tablet's OS preference, where **a device that was never told about dark mode answers "light" at every hour of the night**. That is not a household choosing light; it is nobody answering, and a media query cannot tell those two apart. This is the general failure, not an edge case: it applies to the PWA, a browser pointed straight at the add-on, and any phone shortcut.
- **`panel_theme: 'sun'`** resolves the same question **server-side**, which is the whole trick: the *browser* is the thing that cannot reach Home Assistant from outside the house, while the add-on always can. `services/home_board.sun_theme()` reads **`sun.sun`** through the existing cached REST client and returns a literal `light`/`dark` plus `next_flip`.
- **HA's actual selected theme is deliberately NOT read.** The frontend dark-mode flag lives behind the `frontend/get_themes` **websocket** command and `ha_api` is REST-only; since the household's automation is sun-triggered anyway, mirroring `sun.sun` lands on the same answer without a websocket client to keep alive.
- **`profile()` never ships the word `sun`.** It resolves to the literal before responding, so `ha_theme.html`'s cached `data-panel-theme` and every `[data-panel-theme="light"]` rule in `panel_skin.html` work unchanged and nothing downstream learns a new word. `auto` is left exactly as it was — it is genuinely right inside HA and for a phone on a dark-mode schedule, so this is a fourth option, not a redefinition.
- **Two offsets, not one** (`panel_theme_sunset_offset_minutes`, `panel_theme_sunrise_offset_minutes`, clamped ±240 in the UI).
