- **The report**: on the wall panel, kids repeatedly ended up out of panel mode and on the admin pages just by tapping things. There was not one bad link — the mechanism was wrong.
- **`nav.html` propagated the query string onto `a[href]` exactly once**, in a DOMContentLoaded pass. These pages render almost everything client-side from API data AFTERWARDS, so every link built by a renderer's template string, an Alpine `:href` or a modal opened later was invisible to it. `components/moments_hearth.html` already carried a hand-fix with a comment naming this exact hole; patching links one at a time is how the leak survived.
- **Second hole in the same pass**: the skip was `!link.href.includes('#')`, which exempted every link with a FRAGMENT. `/settings`' rows are `page#anchor` and navigate to a different page, so none of them ever carried the flag. Now only a link that is *nothing but* a fragment is skipped, and the rewrite keeps `url.hash` (it was dropping the anchor).
- **`dashboard.html` linked to `/config` absolutely**, which under Home Assistant ingress does not mean Chauffeur's config at all — the app is served from `/api/hassio_ingress/<token>/`, so that tap left Chauffeur and landed on **Home Assistant's own settings**. Now relative, and `scenario_no_internal_link_is_an_absolute_path` fails on any new one.
- **The fix is two mechanisms in `ha_theme.html`, not more patched links.**
  - **A click-time interceptor** (capture phase on `document`): any same-origin anchor gets the sticky display params — `panel`, `kiosk`, `readonly`, `theme`, `tabs` — rewritten onto its href at the moment it is clicked, so a card rendered five minutes later obeys the same rule as one rendered at load. Capture phase because errands' list chips call `stopPropagation`. It mutates `href` rather than `preventDefault` + assign, because many of these anchors carry their own onclick. Left alone: bare `#fragment`, `javascript:`/`tel:`/`mailto:`, `target` other than `_self`, and anything cross-origin. It sits ABOVE the panel-only early return so kiosk cards and readonly embeds get it too; in an ordinary browser session none of the sticky params are set and every click falls through.
  - **A sessionStorage latch** (`chauffeurPanelSession`): once a TAB has been in panel mode it stays in panel mode, and a URL arriving without the flag has it restored before anything reads it. This is what makes a missed link harmless rather than load-bearing — including `window.location.href = …`, which nothing can intercept. sessionStorage deliberately, not localStorage: the panel's browser session is the panel's whole life, while a laptop that previewed the board in one tab is not latched forever. **`?panel=false` is the escape hatch** and clears the latch — that is how you reach the admin pages *on* the panel on purpose.
- **`window.chfDisplayUrl(href)`** is the shared helper, and it rebuilds from the href AS WRITTEN rather than from `url.pathname`. Every internal link in this app is relative on purpose (ingress again) — round-tripping through `URL` would have absolutised all of them and turned a lost query param into a lost application. Caught by the tests, not by review.
- **The three JS navigations that no interceptor can see** now carry the flags by hand: `trip.html`'s delete → `trips` and its reschedule → `trip?event_id=…`, and `schedule_timeline.html`'s trip-block onclick. `trips.html` and `calendar.html` already carried their own `carryParams`.
- Tests: `tests/test_panel_stickiness.py` (8 scenarios) EXECUTES the ha_theme block against a DOM stub — a link built after load carries the flag, a page anchor keeps both flag and anchor, external/`_blank`/`tel:`/`javascript:` are untouched, an ordinary browser session is untouched, the latch recovers a bare URL, `?panel=false` gets out — plus two static guards: no JS navigation to an internal page without carrying the flags, and no absolute internal `href`.

## A tile is an instance, not a type (configurable board, arc 1 — v2.180.0)

- **The finding**: "two calendar tiles, configured differently" was impossible by CONSTRUCTION, not by omission. A widget's type WAS its identity in six places at once: `panel_widgets` stored type names, `panel_tile_spans` was keyed by type name, the tile payload carried one `key`, Alpine's `x-for` keyed on it, the build cache key was `','.join(keys)`, and `resolve_widgets` deduped — so a second calendar was silently discarded with no way for the household to find out why.
- **A board is now a list of INSTANCES** — `{id, type, config}` — normalised in exactly one place, `home_board.normalize_instances`. `id` is what the board, the grid and the editor address; `type` is what it draws. The tile payload carries `id`, `type` and `config`; `key` is gone rather than left as an ambiguous alias, because a half-migrated name is how this bug class comes back.
- **The id rule is load-bearing for the upgrade**: the FIRST instance of a type takes the bare type as its id (`calendar`, then `calendar-2`, `calendar-3`). Every board that exists stores type names and every stored tile size is keyed by one, so migrated instances already have the right id and their sizes already line up. A uuid would have needed a real data migration to avoid resetting everybody's layout.
- **Upgraded lazily, never rewritten behind anyone's back.** `normalize_instances` accepts both the old list-of-strings and the new list-of-objects; the editor writes the new shape the next time somebody touches the board. `Settings.panel_widgets` is `List[Any]` for the same reason — validating it as instances would reject the shape every existing install has stored.
- **`?widgets=` grew a second grammar.** `?widgets=drives,calendar` is what people type and what every existing HA dashboard card sends, and is unchanged (it can now repeat a type: `map,map` gives `map` and `map-2`). A **JSON array** is what a card sends when it wants a *configured* board — once a tile has options, a comma-separated list of names cannot express what a card wants, and inventing a mini-language in a query string would be worse than admitting it is JSON. Malformed JSON falls back to the defaults rather than emptying the wall.
- **Two singleton DOM ids had to go.** `#board-timeline` and `#board-map` were one element each; a second driving or map tile would have been two elements with the same id, and the renderer would have drawn into whichever the document handed back first. Both now carry the instance (`board-timeline-{id}`, `board-map-{id}`), the CSS that targeted them moved to classes, and `boardMap` became `boardMaps` keyed by instance. **The schedule timeline keeps its state in MODULE variables**, so instances are rendered strictly one at a time in a loop — that is why the component cannot simply be instantiated twice, and it is the constraint any future multi-timeline work has to solve first. Each map tile is a real Leaflet instance with its own tile requests: two is a choice, six is a slideshow, on a Raspberry Pi.
- **The editor now offers every tile every time**, with a count beside any type already on the board, instead of hiding what you had picked — that palette was right when a type could appear once and is the thing being fixed. Rows are keyed by instance id (a type key would have made the second calendar reuse the first one's DOM row and become unreachable), labelled by `config.title` → type label → "(2 of 3)", and removing an instance deletes its stored size so a later instance reusing the id does not inherit it.
- Arc 1 is deliberately a no-op on screen: `config` is accepted and threaded to every builder but nothing reads it yet. Options come in arc 2. Tests: 4 new scenarios in `tests/test_home_board.py` (repeat a type, old shape upgrades with its sizes intact, both `?widgets=` grammars, malformed JSON falls back).

## Tiles answer to their own config (configurable board, arc 2 — v2.181.0)

- **A tile type DECLARES what it can be asked**, in `home_board.WIDGETS`, next to the builder that reads it. The editor renders those declarations; it knows five shapes (`text`, `int`, `bool`, `choice`, `select`) and nothing about calendars or drivers. The pair that drifts apart in a system like this is "what the editor offers" and "what the builder honours", and one list is the only real defence — `tests/test_board_instances.py` fails on a declared shape the editor cannot draw, and on a `select` whose source `option_sources()` does not supply.
- **`select` options draw from live household data** — members, drivers, shopping lists — shipped in the catalog as `{value, label}` rows. Each source is fetched independently and **independently survives failure**: a board editor that will not open because the shopping service threw is worse than one picker being empty.
- **Empty multi-select means EVERYONE, on every tile that takes one.** A filter that shows nothing until you have ticked somebody reads as broken rather than as unconfigured.
- **`title` is offered on every type**, appended in `catalog()` rather than declared fifteen times, and an instance's own title beats the catalog's label. A board with three calendar tiles all called "What's coming" cannot be read.
- **Defaults that are deliberately `null`.** The calendar's `days` has no literal default: absent means `panel_agenda_days`, so a tile nobody configured tracks the board setting *including when the household changes it later*. The editor enforces the other half — **a value equal to its default stops being stored**, so opening an options panel and closing it does not write out a config of numbers nobody chose and quietly pin the tile.
- **Calendar**: `view` (a card per day / one list), `days` (1–14), `members` (matched via `FamilyMember.calendar_ids`, the one place a person's calendars are set — a member with no calendars matches nothing, which is correct rather than a bug to paper over), `all_day`. A filtered tile **drops errands**: an errand is household work with a driver, not something on a person's calendar, and "Emma's week" containing the trip to the tip is worse than it missing it. Its empty state names whose calendar is quiet, because "nothing on the calendar" under a tile headed "Emma's week" reads as the tile being broken.
- **Drives**: `view` (timeline / list), `days`, `drivers`, `errands`. The timeline is the Drives page's own renderer and draws **exactly one day**, so a tile asked for a week cannot be one — the day count belongs to the list view and its help text says so, rather than being a setting that silently does nothing. The driver filter prunes the timeline's **lanes only, never its events**: a timeline with the day's other drives removed reads as a free afternoon. The same filter is applied to `runs` so the count, the scroll target and the drawing agree about whose day it is.
- **Lists**: `list` (one, or all) and `items` (0–20; 0 shows counts only). `items` defaults to **12** because that is what the tile already showed — a smaller default would have been a silent redesign of every board. A tile pinned to a list somebody later deleted says *"That list is gone."*: falling back to all lists would look like the setting doing nothing, hiding the tile would look like the board being broken.
- **The prefetch now reaches as deep as the DEEPEST tile**, not as deep as `panel_agenda_days`. Each day is merged in from its own cache row, so a fortnight-long tile against a five-day prefetch rendered nine empty days and looked like a quiet calendar rather than a board that had not read that far.
- Tests: `tests/test_board_instances.py` (15 scenarios) covers both halves — the builders through `build()` with real config, and the editor's own `setCfg`/`toggleCfgId`/`removeInstance` RUN in node, because "a value equal to the default stops being stored" is exactly the rule that looks obviously right and is obviously wrong the first time somebody opens a panel and closes it.

## Every tile answers to its instance (configurable board, arc 3 — v2.182.0)

- **All fifteen types now declare options** (intake carries only `title` — it is a count and there is nothing else to ask it). The editor was already generic, so this arc is almost entirely declaration plus the matching read in each builder.
- **Kids**: which children, and how many lines each. **Meals**: `nights` and `offset` — one night is "what is on the table" and reads as a picture of dinner, several is "what is planned" and reads as a list with a day on each card. Same data; the only difference is how many dates were asked for, so it is one number rather than a view switch, and the day label is only sent when there is more than one night to tell apart. **Chores / Routines**: which people, how many rows. **Occasions**: rows, and `within_days`. **Weather**: days. **Moments**: how many and how far back. **Errands**: rows, past-due only. **Tasks**: rows, owner, unclaimed-only. **Trips**: pin one trip or list the next N. **Map**: which people, and whether cars show.
- **A pinned trip that has ended says so** — *"That trip is over."* — the same rule the pinned shopping list follows: silently showing a different trip under a tile the household set to one is worse than saying the pin is stale. A pinned trip also gets its location line, because a tile about one trip can afford detail a list of four cannot.
- **`unclaimed_only` and an owner filter are opposite questions.** An unclaimed task has no owner and can never match an owner filter, so a tile given both honours unclaimed-only; returning nothing would look broken rather than contradictory.
- **Two guards earn their keep here.** `scenario_every_declared_option_survives_its_own_builder` sweeps every type with every option set to an extreme at once (max for a number, opposite for a switch, last choice, an id matching nothing) — because a builder that raises is swallowed by `build()` and the tile just *vanishes from the wall*, the failure mode with no error anywhere. `scenario_a_configured_tile_is_still_the_tile_it_was` pins every row-cap default to what the tile showed before the option existed: chores 6, routines 6, occasions 3, errands 5, tasks 6, moments 6, trips 4, weather 5, lists 12. A tile quietly showing six rows where it showed five is a redesign of every board in the wild delivered as a bug fix.
- **`option_sources()` gained `trips`**, deduped across cached trips and drafts, and like the other three it fails independently — one dead source empties one picker rather than refusing to open the editor.
- Arc 3 closes the configurable-board work: a board is a list of instances, every instance has an id, a size, a title and its type's own options, and the same type can appear as many times as the household wants. **Not done, and deliberately:** Home Assistant entity/camera tiles (arc 4 as scoped, not built), and multiple *timeline* driving tiles — `components/schedule_timeline.html` keeps its state in module variables, so instances are drawn one at a time and two different days cannot be drawn concurrently. That constraint is what any future multi-timeline work has to solve first.

## A driving tile draws one timeline PER DAY (v2.183.0 — corrects v2.182.0)

- **The correction.** v2.182.0 claimed the drives tile could only ever be one day, on the grounds that "the timeline is the Drives page's own renderer and draws exactly one day". That is wrong. `renderSchedule` seeds `scheduleByDate` from `currentStartDate`..`currentEndDate` and then loops `sortedDates.forEach`, drawing one **complete timeline section per day** into its container — which is exactly how the Schedule page shows a week in kiosk mode. Multi-day was never missing.
- **The tile was narrowing it away itself.** It passed `dateFilter: data.date`, and `dateFilter` is the option that filters `sortedDates` back down to a single date. Remove it, set the range, and the same renderer draws the week.
- **So `days` now applies to the timeline view**, not just the list. `_schedule_slice` takes a `days` count and returns the whole range; the tile sends `days`/`start_date`/`end_date`; the client sets `currentStartDate`/`currentEndDate` from them and **withholds `dateFilter` when the span is more than one**. Either half alone silently renders today and looks like the setting doing nothing, so both are asserted.
- **Empty days are skipped, and that is the Schedule page's rule rather than a gap here**: in read-only mode a driver column with no real events is hidden, and a date left with no columns is not drawn at all. A three-day tile with drives on two of them draws two sections. This is what sharing the renderer *means* — the tile inherits the kiosk behaviour instead of reimplementing it.
- **The list view stays, with its meaning corrected.** It is a WIDTH answer, not a span answer: a tile too narrow to read an hour rail in can still say the same thing as a compact list, and `days` works in both. The option help no longer claims the day count is list-only.
- **What is still true about the module-state constraint**, stated more precisely than before: `components/schedule_timeline.html` keeps `currentData`, `currentStartDate/EndDate` and `lastScheduleRenderOptions` in module variables. That does **not** limit one tile to one day. It limits two *instances* holding different data — they are drawn sequentially in a loop, which is fine, but the optional-event handler's `renderSchedule(currentData, lastScheduleRenderOptions)` re-render would redraw the last instance's data. Two driving tiles with different configs is the case to be careful with; multi-day within one tile is not.
- Tests: `scenario_a_multi_day_driving_tile_draws_a_timeline_per_day` in `tests/test_board_render.py` asserts it in a real DOM — three days configured, drives on two, two day headings drawn, and the drive two days out actually present. Asserting the payload alone would have passed for the broken version too. `tests/test_board_instances.py` covers the range slice and pins both halves of the client contract.

## Home Assistant on the board (configurable board, arc 4 — v2.184.0)

- **Two new tile types, neither on by default.** `ha` shows any entities the household picks as a list of readings; `ha_image` shows a camera or image entity as the tile. Neither is in `DEFAULT_WIDGETS`: a household without HA should never be shown a tile about it, and one with HA has to choose which entities matter — there is no sensible default set of somebody else's sensors.
- **Not Lovelace cards, and not pretending to be.** HA's cards are custom elements needing a live `hass` object and an authenticated websocket; there is no supported way to run one here. What travels is the DATA — entity, state, unit — rendered in the panel's own vocabulary, which reads better on this wall than an embedded rectangle of somebody else's theme.
- **The degradation rule, and the correction that made it right.** The first cut said *"Needs Home Assistant"* when HA was absent. That reads fine alone and quietly broke **property 1** — "an unconfigured feature has no tile", the rule that hides the shopping tile from a household that never made a list. `tests/test_home_board.py` caught it. The split is now explicit: `ha_configured()` (configuration only, instant) means the feature exists at all → **no HA configured returns None, the tile does not exist**; `ha_available()` (configured AND answering) means a set-up feature is quiet → **"Needs Home Assistant."**. Configured but no entities picked → *"Pick some entities in board setup."*
- **The hand path for that rule**: the palette marks both tiles `available: false` with `requires: 'Home Assistant'` and renders them disabled, so nobody can add a tile that could never draw and then wonder why the wall never shows it.
- **`ha_available()` never makes a request when `mode()` already said unconfigured.** A household with no Home Assistant must not wait on a timeout to draw a board that has nothing to do with Home Assistant.
- **A missing entity is NAMED, not dropped** — "not in HA" beside the row. A row quietly disappearing from a wall is how a household stops trusting the wall, and naming it is the only version anybody acts on.
- **Tapping is OFF by default** (`interactive`), and that is board rule 3 rather than timidity: a display does not change what it is displaying. Somebody adding this tile to read a temperature must not get light switches. **Two allowlists, not one**: the tile decides what to draw a control for, `POST /api/ha/toggle` decides what may actually be operated. `HA_TOGGLE_DOMAINS` is `light`, `switch`, `fan`, `input_boolean` — a wall panel in a kitchen is reachable by everybody in the house including the people who cannot read yet, and `lock`, `cover` and `alarm_*` are not things a mis-tap or a crafted request should work. The endpoint returns no state (`get_state` reads a 5s cache and HA may not have settled); the client flips optimistically and the next board poll makes it true.
- **A sixth option shape, `entity`.** `select` renders every option as a chip, which is right for six members and unusable for the two thousand entities an ordinary HA has. `entity` is a text input with a `<datalist>`, fed by `GET /api/home_board/ha_options` **lazily, on first focus** — those entities are deliberately NOT in the catalog, which every browser loads to edit a board. `available: false` is the editor's whole degradation contract: a picker that knows HA is absent says so, rather than showing an empty list indistinguishable from "no matches".
- **The picture is a URL, never bytes.** The board payload is refetched every 60 seconds; a tile that inlined camera frames would drag a JPEG through every poll. `/api/camera_proxy/` joined `_HA_IMAGE_PREFIXES` — the same KIND of thing as the three already there, and the allowlist stays an allowlist. Cache-busting rides the clock that is already ticking rather than adding a second interval.
- **A tile with no page is not a link.** Every other tile is an `<a>` to the page it summarises; these summarise nothing of ours. The anchor's `href` binds to `null` for pageless types — Alpine drops the attribute, an anchor without `href` is not interactive content, and it legally contains the toggle buttons. (A dynamic tag name is a Vue idea; Alpine has nothing like it, and duplicating 400 lines of tile body under two `x-if`s to change one tag would be worse than either.)
- Tests: `tests/test_board_ha_tiles.py` (18 scenarios), weighted at the no-HA half — absent vs down vs unconfigured-tile, the palette's refusal, no network call when unconfigured, the missing entity, both allowlists, the picture-is-a-URL rule, and tapping being off until asked for.

## The camera tile had nowhere to draw, and dashboards can be framed (v2.185.0)

- **Why cameras rendered nothing.** Not the fetch. `ha_image` was missing from `DRAWN_TILES`, so `fillsTile()` said false and the tile card got `block tile-scroll` instead of `flex flex-col`. The picture's container is `flex-1 min-h-0` holding an absolutely positioned `<img>` — in a non-flex parent `flex-1` does nothing, the box is **zero pixels tall**, and the photograph has nowhere to go. No error, no console line, a heading over nothing: the board's signature failure mode, third time. `ha_image` and `ha_dashboard` are drawn tiles now, and `tests/test_board_ha_tiles.py` asserts membership rather than trusting it.
- **Cameras no longer depend on a rotating token.** A camera's `entity_picture` carries `?token=`, which Home Assistant rotates every few minutes; the board's payload is cached and then cached again by the browser, so a panel can be holding a URL whose token has expired. The tile now points at `/api/camera_proxy/<entity_id>` directly — the authenticated endpoint, which needs no token because the proxy hop already carries our bearer. `image.*` entities keep using `entity_picture`. A missing entity now says *"That entity is not in Home Assistant."* rather than *"has no picture"*: those send a household looking in two different places.
- **`ha_dashboard` — a real Home Assistant dashboard view, framed.** This is the honest answer to "render HA cards". A Lovelace card is a custom element wanting a live `hass` object and an authenticated websocket; it cannot run here. HA rendering it in a frame can, and **one card on its own dashboard view is the shape that makes this work** — which is exactly what the household asked for. Options: `path` (`lovelace/0`, `my-panel/cameras`, or a full URL) and `refresh_seconds` (default **0** — a dashboard is live already, and reloading only costs a flash of white).
- **It comes with a frame's rules, and they are in the blurb, not just the code.** It is HA's look, not the panel's, and it will not take the panel theme. It only works where the browser is signed in to HA — and HA sends `X-Frame-Options: SAMEORIGIN`, so **under ingress it works (the panel is already on HA's origin) and a standalone panel on :8000 is a different origin and will very likely be refused however well the URL is spelled.**
- **`ha_browser_url` is a new setting and a genuinely different question from `ha_base_url`.** Under the Supervisor the server's route is `http://supervisor/core` — correct for us, meaningless in a browser. Empty is the default and the useful one: a root-relative `/lovelace/0` resolves to HA under ingress and is same-origin. It lives in the panel-setup drawer next to the board, not on the config page, because that is where somebody is standing when they add the tile. `same_origin` is computed from the RESOLVED url, not from whether the setting is filled in — a `path` typed as a full `https://` address is cross-origin however empty the setting is.
- **The frame is not sandboxed, deliberately.** A dashboard needs its own scripts, websocket and storage; sandboxing produces a blank frame and a mysterious console error. It is HA's page on HA's origin — not a place to apply our containment.
- Tests: `tests/test_board_ha_tiles.py` grew to 25 scenarios, including the drawn-tile membership that would have caught the camera bug on the day, the token-free camera path, and all three HA tiles under the no-HA rules.

## A framed dashboard without Home Assistant's chrome (v2.186.0)

- **Stock Home Assistant has no query parameter for this.** `?kiosk` belongs to the **kiosk-mode** integration (HACS), which is worth installing and which this tile already supports for free — `path` is free text, so `lovelace/0?kiosk` works today and the cache-buster appends `&v=` correctly around an existing query string. The tile's help says so.
- **What we can do without it**: the frame is SAME-ORIGIN under ingress, so its document is reachable and a stylesheet can be inserted into it. HA's frontend is a tree of web components, so a parent stylesheet cannot reach the header — that is what a shadow root is for. The style has to go **into each shadow root that owns the thing being hidden**, which is what kiosk-mode does too: `home-assistant → home-assistant-main` (sidebar, `--mdc-drawer-width: 0`) and `→ partial-panel-resolver → ha-panel-lovelace → hui-root` (`.header`, `app-header`, `.toolbar`).
- **New `chrome` option, defaulting to `hide`** — the difference between "a card on the wall" and "a browser window on the wall". `as_is` leaves the dashboard alone.
- **It is skipped, not attempted, cross-origin.** Reading a cross-origin frame's document throws a SecurityError into the console on every load of a tile that was already never going to work; the tile checks `same_origin` first.
- **The retry is BOUNDED** — 12 attempts, 250ms apart, stopping on success. HA builds its component tree after `load`, so the first attempt misses; a MutationObserver inside a live dashboard would run for as long as the panel is up, on a Raspberry Pi, for three seconds' worth of benefit. The injected `<style>` persists in the shadow root once inserted, and re-inserting is guarded by id so a reload cannot stack stylesheets.
- **This depends on Home Assistant's internal element names and will need revisiting when its frontend is restructured.** That is an acceptable trade for a wall panel and a bad one for anything load-bearing: when it stops working the dashboard simply comes back with its header — a visible, harmless, self-explaining failure.
- Tests: `tests/test_board_ha_tiles.py` at 29 scenarios. The stripper is EXECUTED against a jsdom stand-in with HA's nesting and real shadow roots (did the style land in the right root, and does a second run stack a second sheet), and — because that harness builds the tree it expects and would stay green through a rename — the element names are separately pinned as literals, so an HA restructure fails a test that names what to go and look at.

## Hiding the sidebar left the hole it sat in (v2.186.1)

- **Reported straight after v2.186.0**: the sidebar went, the empty column it occupied stayed. `?kiosk` (the kiosk-mode integration) gets this right, so it was clearly possible.
- **The space is not the sidebar's.** It is a MARGIN on the content beside it, carried by `.mdc-drawer-app-content` — and that element lives inside **`ha-drawer`'s own shadow root**, one level below the root the sidebar rule is injected into. The first cut aimed the margin rule at `home-assistant-main`'s shadow root, where the class does not exist, so it matched nothing and looked like it had worked: sidebar gone, gap intact.
- **The traversal grew a hop.** `main.shadowRoot → ha-drawer → .shadowRoot` gets `#chf-no-gap`, zeroing `margin-left`/`margin-right`/`margin-inline-*` and forcing `width: 100%` on `.mdc-drawer-app-content`, plus hiding `.mdc-drawer` and `.mdc-drawer-scrim`. `app-drawer-layout` is accepted as a fallback for older frontends. `--mdc-drawer-width: 0px` stays on main's `:host` as belt-and-braces: it is what Material's own stylesheet would consume, and HA's build does not always route the margin through it, so the margin is also zeroed directly in the root that owns it.
- **The harness was the reason this shipped.** It modelled `home-assistant-main → partial-panel-resolver` and had no `ha-drawer` at all, so there was no element carrying the margin and nothing to notice was missing. It now includes `ha-drawer` with a real shadow root and a `.mdc-drawer-app-content` inside it — and, correctly, `partial-panel-resolver` as a LIGHT-DOM child of the drawer (it is slotted, so it lives in main's shadow tree and `mainRoot.querySelector` finds it). A harness that disagrees with the app's own traversal proves nothing; getting that nesting wrong made the stripper "fail" against a tree HA does not have.
- Tests: 29 scenarios, with the gap asserted separately from the sidebar because they are separate failures, and `ha-drawer`/`mdc-drawer-app-content` added to the pinned element names.

## A real Home Assistant card, running in a tile (v2.187.0)

- **The `ha_dashboard` frame was built on a claim that was too strong.** "A Lovelace card is a custom element wanting a live `hass` object and an authenticated websocket; it cannot run here" — the second half is what did the work, and it is wrong for most cards worth putting on a wall. `tesla-style-solar-power-card` touches exactly one field of `hass` (`states`, five times) and no websocket at all. `power-flow-card-plus` adds `localize`, `user` and `connection`. Both are ordinary custom elements.
- **New tile: `ha_card`.** Paste the same YAML Home Assistant shows you under *Show code editor*. The server parses it (`services/ha_cards.py`), reads the entity ids out of the config, ships only those states inside the existing board payload, and finds the card's own file in HA's registered Lovelace resources. The browser half (`static/ha_card_host.js`) fetches that file through a proxy, defines the HA elements the card renders into, and hands it a `hass`.
- **Why this beats the frame: an HA theme is nothing but CSS custom properties, and they cross shadow boundaries.** Mapping `--primary-text-color → --panel-fg`, `--primary-color → --panel-accent`, `--divider-color → --panel-line` (and `--ha-card-background: transparent`, no shadow — the tile brings the surface) makes a card that never heard of Chauffeur draw itself in the panel's colours, in light and dark, with no fork of its code. The semantic colours (`--success-color` and friends) deliberately stay literals: green means the same thing on every wall, and repainting it in the accent colour would erase what the card is saying.
- **The limit is BUILT-IN cards, and it is stated by name.** `type: gauge` resolves to `hui-gauge-card`, compiled into HA's frontend bundle — there is no file to fetch and never will be. Only `type: custom:…` ships as a resource. The YAML for the two looks identical, so the tile answers *"`gauge` is one of Home Assistant's built-in cards…"* rather than failing to load, which would send somebody hunting for a file that was never the problem. Built-in types need a config→native converter instead — a different and easier job.
- **Also out of reach**: cards calling `loadCardHelpers()` or extending `hui-*` classes (they never define; the tile says so after 8s rather than spinning), the statistics websocket the official energy cards use (`subscribeMessage` REJECTS — a promise left unsettled is a permanent spinner on a wall), and card visual editors, which want `ha-form`/`ha-entity-picker`. All of it falls back to the framed `ha_dashboard` tile, which is why both exist.
- **Proven against the real bundles, not a mock.** `tesla-style-solar-power-card` 1.4 and `power-flow-card-plus` 0.3.7, downloaded untouched, mounted in jsdom with no Home Assistant anywhere: 6.8 KB and 17 KB of shadow DOM, the `ha-card` shim used, `mdi:solar-panel-large` requested from the icon endpoint, and the fed states on screen (`3.3 kW`, `87 %`). Two failures in that run were jsdom's, not ours — `getTotalLength` (unimplemented SVG geometry, so **the flow ANIMATION is unverified**; the static render is not) and `ResizeObserver`. See `docs/ha_card_hosting.md`.
- **Icons, at last.** `/api/ha/card/mdi/{name}` reads HA's own MDI chunks (`/static/mdi/iconMetadata.json` → alphabetical chunk), so the icon set cannot drift from the one the household sees in HA. Names are validated `^[a-z0-9-]+$` before they reach HA; chunks and misses are both cached. This retires the constraint recorded next to `_HA_DOMAIN_GLYPH` — the app can draw an mdi icon now, and the plain entity tile's per-domain emoji could use it.
- **The proxy's allowlist is its whole security story.** `/api/ha/card/resource` fetches with the supervisor token, so it serves `/hacsfiles/`, `/local/` and `/community_plugin/` only, and refuses absolute URLs outright — a resource on a CDN is something the browser can load itself, and routing it through an authenticated proxy buys nothing. `/api/ha/card/service` reuses the entity tile's domain allowlist; the tile's own "let the card control things" switch defaults **off** and is enforced in the browser too, because a hosted card is a stranger's code on a screen in a kitchen.
- Tests: `tests/test_ha_cards.py` (9 scenarios — built-ins refused by name, the path allowlist including traversal and absolute URLs, resource matching, entity extraction from nested configs, the named-not-dropped missing entity, no-HA as a sentence) and `tests/test_ha_card_host_runtime.py` (3 — the theme map as a contract, read-only refused before the request leaves, statistics rejected not hung). `test_board_instances.py` now READS the editor's option shapes out of `home.html` instead of hard-coding six, so a new shape cannot pass a stale list.

## A card's file is not an API path (v2.187.1)

- **Reported on the first real install**: *"Home Assistant would not hand over that card's file."* The card was at `/config/www/community/tesla-style-solar-power-card` and had resolved correctly — the tile found the right URL and then could not fetch it.
- **`http://supervisor/core` is a proxy to the API, not to Home Assistant.** It forwards `/core/api` and `/core/websocket`. `ha_api.fetch_binary` builds `{supervisor}/core{path}`, so `/hacsfiles/…` and `/local/…` resolved to a route the Supervisor does not serve. **Every previous caller of `fetch_binary` passes an `/api/…` path** (`main._HA_IMAGE_PREFIXES` is four `/api/` prefixes), which is exactly why the limit was invisible until something asked for a static file.
- **New `ha_api.core_origins()` / `fetch_static()`.** Add-ons share a network with the Core container, which answers on `homeassistant:8123` — the route to anything HA serves that is not the API. Tokenless on purpose: `/local/`, `/hacsfiles/` and `/static/` are served **without authentication** (HA's own frontend has to load before anyone signs in), and the supervisor token would not be valid against Core directly anyway. External/dev mode falls through to `ha_base_url` minus its `/api`.
- **Icons had the same bug** — `/static/mdi/…` is frontend static content too, so it went through the same wrong door and would have quietly drawn nothing.
- **The script-tag failure now names the path it tried.** A `<script>` error event carries no status and no body, so the only useful thing the message can add is where it looked.
- Tests: `tests/test_ha_cards.py` at 11 scenarios. Two of them assert **which door** is used rather than what comes back — a card's file and an mdi chunk must both go through `fetch_static`, and `core_origins()` must not contain the supervisor proxy at all.

## The row height is a number (v2.187.2)

- **Reported against a hosted card**: the bottom of it clips, and no choice in the row-height menu fixes it. The menu offered seven sizes (120–400) — never a technical limit. `grid-auto-rows` takes any integer and the server has always clamped **80–600**, so the dropdown could not reach two thirds of the valid range, while sitting between two number inputs (columns, agenda days) in the same card.
- **Now `<input type="number" min=80 max=600 step=5>`.** The save path's clamp already matched the server's, so nothing else moved. A tile that needs 250 can have 250.

## More than one board (v2.188.0) — pages arc, slice 1

- **A board stopped being THE board.** Everything before this assumed one wall panel showing one set of tiles, and the settings said so: `panel_widgets`, `panel_tile_spans`, `panel_grid_columns`, `panel_grid_row_height` were single household-wide keys. Instanceable tiles pointing at specific HA entities are what broke it — a driveway camera belongs on the hallway panel and nowhere else.
- **New `panel_pages`**: `{slug, name, icon, widgets, spans, columns, row_height, background}` per board. **The migration is lazy and the old keys are what the FIRST page is built from** when the list is empty — same discipline as the instance-shape upgrade, so a household that never opens the editor keeps its board byte for byte: same tiles, same order, same sizes, same grid, same picture.
- **`panel_pages` wins ENTIRELY once it exists.** A board reading its columns from a page and its row height from a legacy key is a split brain nobody can debug, so there is no such state — and the editor stops sending the legacy keys rather than writing both.
- **`/board/{slug}`, and an unknown slug is not a 404.** The address that produces one is a wall panel's bookmark pointing at a board somebody deleted; a screen bolted to a wall showing an error page is worse than the same screen showing the home board. `find_page` falls back. `home` stays reserved and stays at `/home` — it is the panel's resting state and the shelf's Home button, and moving it would break every existing bookmark and HA card.
- **An empty board stays empty.** `DEFAULT_WIDGETS` exists so a household that never configured anything gets a full board rather than a blank wall; a board somebody just MADE is a deliberate act, and filling it with thirteen tiles would be the app arguing with it.
- **The board cache is keyed by board now, not one slot.** Two panels on two boards would have evicted each other on every poll — each request a full rebuild, the cache doing worse than nothing. Bounded at 8 with least-recently-built eviction, because the key includes tile config and a card cycling `?widgets=` could otherwise grow it forever.
- **`apiBase` had to learn about depth.** `/board/kitchen` is one segment below `/home`, and the fix is counted AGAINST `/home` rather than from the root — counting real path segments breaks under ingress, where `/api/hassio_ingress/<token>/` sits in front of everything and must not be climbed out of.
- **The editor is page-first**: a chip per board, New/rename/delete, the board's address printed under its name (a wall panel is a bookmark — the thing you type into a tablet has to be copyable), and every tile control now addresses the selected page. The home board is offered as un-deletable rather than as a delete that mysteriously fails.
- Tests: `tests/test_board_pages.py` (11 scenarios), weighted at the migration — the existing board's tiles, order, config, sizes, grid and picture all surviving; nothing written back on a read; no half-migrated state; a deleted board landing on a real one; slugs unique and URL-safe; and end-to-end that a payload carries the grid of the board it was built for. `test_board_instances.py`'s editor probe now seeds a page.
